<meta>This page begins the introduction to the collection, following the Table of Contents. It establishes the historical context of the work and introduces the first author, Henry Cornelius Agrippa.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

*It is surprising how often it is claimed that The Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy is fake <note>original: "spurious"</note>. This is repeated by one "authority" after another, clearly without any reference to the text itself. This volume is not so much a single book as it is a collection of six treatises on various aspects of practical magic and divination. A glance at the table of contents will confirm that only the first two treatises actually claim to be written by Henry Cornelius Agrippa.*

*Agrippa (1486–1535) was in many ways a well-rounded Renaissance man, being a writer, soldier, and physician. However, his main claim to an important place in the history of thought during this period is as a magician. This is by no means a belittlement of his other achievements, for as Agrippa himself says:*

> "Some who are perverse... may take the name of Magic in its worst sense and, though having barely seen the title, cry out that I teach forbidden arts, sow the seeds of heresies, offend pious ears, and scandalize excellent minds <note>original: "wits"</note>; that I am a sorcerer, and superstitious and devilish, when I am indeed a magician. To them I answer that among learned men, a magician does not signify a sorcerer, or one who is superstitious or devilish, but a wise man, a priest, and a prophet."

*Turning to the contents of this volume, let us examine each treatise in turn:*

## I. On Geomancy <note>A method of divination interpreting patterns formed by earth, stones, or sand.</note> — Henry Cornelius Agrippa

*It is likely this treatise that Agrippa mentioned in 1526 when he sent to Metz for his work on geomancy. Agrippa refers again to a work on geomancy in his *On the Uncertainty and Vanity of the Sciences* <note>original: "De Incertitudine," Chapter 13</note>. There, after listing earlier works on geomancy by Haly, Gerard of Cremona, Bartholomew of Parma, and Tundinus, he says of his own geomantic treatise, "I too have*

<summary>This introduction defends the authenticity of the collection's contents and introduces Henry Cornelius Agrippa, defining his view of a "magician" as a wise and holy figure rather than a common sorcerer.</summary>
<keywords>Henry Cornelius Agrippa, Occult Philosophy, Magic vs Sorcery, Geomancy, Renaissance Thought, De Incertitudine, practical magic</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction's analysis of the specific treatises included in the collection, moving from the context of Agrippa's self-criticism to the details of his work on geomancy and the controversial "Fourth Book."</meta>

*...written a geomancy quite different from the rest but no less superstitious and fallacious—or if you wish, I will even say ‘lying’ <note>original: "mendacious"</note>." Agrippa was nothing if not honest about his own work!*

*From the 12th to the 17th century, geomancy was one of the major forms of divination in Europe. It ranked second only to astrology but was held in higher regard than the tarot. The first part of this book deals with the mechanics of geomantic divination; the second part concerns their application in an astrological context. The third and by far the largest part focuses on the meanings of each of the sixteen geomantic figures within each of the twelve astrological houses <note>original: "Houses of heaven"</note>.*

*This text on geomancy is extremely interesting because it provides much of the source material for later works derived from this subject. Interestingly, it greatly expands upon the remarks Agrippa made on the topic in his Three Books of Occult Philosophy.*

*The actual practice of geomancy is presented here in a very condensed form. For example, the table on page 6 is not very clear, and the diagram on the following page suffers from poor printing quality <note>original: "deficiencies of the printer’s art"</note>. To preserve the quality of the original reproduction <note>original: "facsimile"</note>, these have not been changed. Modern reconstructions of these, along with complete instructions for practicing geomancy, appear in the current author's book, The Oracle of Geomancy (Warner Destiny, New York, 1977), and a history of the subject can be found in Divinatory Geomancy.*

### II. On Occult Philosophy, or On Magical Ceremonies:
### The Fourth Book — Henry Cornelius Agrippa

*This treatise, which was published in Latin about thirty years after Agrippa’s death, is essentially a self-contained book of magic <note>term: "grimoire"</note> or a grammar of sorcery. It relies on the Three Books of Occult Philosophy for its theoretical foundation.*

*Johannes Weir, who served for a time as Agrippa’s student and secretary <note>term: "amanuensis"</note>, stated in one of his many lengthy works that this treatise was not in his master's style. However, elsewhere he admits that Agrippa was so productive <note>original: "prolific"</note> that it was impossible to be certain which of the works published after his death <note>term: "posthumously published"</note> were actually written by him.*

*After an initial exploration into an astrological system...*

<summary>This page discusses the historical significance of geomancy as a divination tool and introduces "The Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy," noting the scholarly debate over whether Henry Cornelius Agrippa actually wrote it.</summary>
<keywords>Geomancy, Astrology, Tarot, Henry Cornelius Agrippa, Three Books of Occult Philosophy, Johannes Weir, grimoire, divination, astrological houses, amanuensis</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s "Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy," specifically detailing the practical methods for spirit conjuration, before introducing the next treatise in the collection: the "Heptameron" by Peter de Abano.</meta>

*To generate the names of good and evil spirits, Agrippa continues by discussing the sizes <note>original: "magnitudes"</note> of the stars and their symbols. A series of characters representing both good and evil spirits follows, organized according to their rank and status.*

*The recognizable forms <note>original: "familiar shapes"</note> of the spirits of the various planets are described <note>original: "delineated"</note> to help identify a specific spirit with its planetary ruler. Agrippa then explains how to create pentacles <note>A "pentacle" is a physical talisman or disk inscribed with magical symbols used in rituals</note> and which signs should be used in their preparation. The design of these pentacles resembles those found in the *Key of Solomon* <note>A famous grimoire or book of magic traditionally attributed to King Solomon</note>, where an image representing a Biblical or visionary <note>original: "Apocalyptic"</note> theme is drawn and then surrounded by a relevant scripture verse and a name of God.*

*The work then covers the specifics of consecrating the various tools necessary for the magical arts, including the types of evocations <note>original: "conjurations"</note>, ointments <note>original: "unctions"</note>, incense-burnings <note>original: "suffumigations"</note>, prayers, and blessings to be used. There is special emphasis on the consecration of water, fire, oils, and perfumes. He provides details for consecrating the magic circle, which serves as the most holy place <note>original Latin: "sanctum sanctorum"</note> for the practice of magic, and explains how to use the *Book of Spirits* <note>original Latin: "Liber Spirituum"</note>. This book must be inscribed with the image and mystical seal <note>original: "sigil"</note> of the spirit, along with the oath that the spirit must take when it is summoned <note>original: "conjured"</note>.*

*Finally, Agrippa reaches the specific instructions for actually summoning <note>original: "invocation"</note> spirits. He describes the kind of location where the ceremony must take place, the necessary preparation and mental state of the magician <note>original: "Magus"</note>, the divine names to be worn on the clothing, and the specific days and times when the operation is permitted. Furthermore, Agrippa explains dream oracles and the tablets and charms <note>original: "talismans"</note> required <note>original: "requisite"</note> for this craft, thereby covering various forms of summoning that do not require a magic circle, and dealing with nature spirits as well as the spirits found in traditional magic books <note>original: "grimoires"</note>.*

## III. Heptameron: or, Magical Elements — Peter de Abano

*Peter of Abano (1250—1317) was one of the most influential scholars <note>original: "men of learning"</note> of his time. Many of his writings on medicine, philosophy, and astronomy still exist <note>original: "extant"</note>, along with works on geomancy <note>A method of divination using patterns in the earth or soil</note> and magic often credited to him, though the latter may be fake <note>original: "spurious"</note>. Nevertheless, it is easier to treat the *Heptameron* (or *Magical Elements*) and the *Geomantia* as his own work, especially since he maintained a lucrative medical practice.*

<summary>This page details the ritual instructions found in Agrippa's Fourth Book—including the creation of pentacles and the consecration of tools—and introduces the influential medieval scholar Peter de Abano.</summary>
<keywords>Agrippa, Peter de Abano, Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy, Heptameron, pentacles, sigils, Book of Spirits, ritual magic, consecration, planetary spirits</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Peter of Abano and his contributions to occult literature, specifically his biographical background and the structure of his most famous work, the Heptameron.</meta>

*And since he had a place in society to maintain, it is likely that the treatise mentioned above remained in manuscript form until sometime after his death, especially as he was in some trouble with the Inquisition <note>The Roman Catholic system for identifying and suppressing heresy</note>.*

*He studied medicine in Paris before returning to Padua to practice as a physician. Toward the end of his life, he was actually accused of practicing sorcery by the Inquisition and was imprisoned. He was later found not guilty <note>original: "acquitted"</note> but was then re-arrested and died in prison in 1317 while awaiting trial.*

*Among the less respectable <note>original: "salubrious"</note> works he wrote was a book on poisons, commissioned by the Pope at the time, possibly Pope Honorius IV.*

*Of the books of magic attributed to Peter, the Heptameron <note>From the Greek for "Seven Days"</note> is the best known. However, the scholar Naudé states that two of his other books were banned after his death: The Necromantic Expositor <note>original: "Elucidarium Necromanticum"</note> and the Book of Marvelous Experiments with Rings according to the 28 Mansions of the Moon <note>original: "Liber Experimentorum Mirabilium de Annulis Secundum 28 Mansiones Lunae"</note>.*

*The Heptameron, which borrows heavily from the Picatrix <note>An influential Arabic handbook of talismanic magic</note> (a magical text by the Arab author pseudo-Maǧrīṭī), may have first appeared in Latin at Venice in 1496 before being bound with Agrippa's work in 1565. The Heptameron has well earned its reputation as a key work on practical magic. It follows the tradition of Johannes Trithemius’ Steganographia <note>A 15th-century work that combined secret writing with the summoning of spirits</note> in that it lists the names of many angels and the specific times to call upon <note>original: "conjuration"</note> them.*

*He begins his book on the magical principles <note>original: "elements"</note> by describing the composition of the circle, which is described as a specific fortress to keep the practitioner safe from evil spirits. The names of the hours and the angels ruling them follow his brief description of the circle, providing the names for each season and when they begin and end.*

*Next follow the ceremonies for consecration and blessing to be performed before the magical operation, including the ritual purification <note>original: "exorcism"</note> of the fire, the making of the special clothes to be worn, and the pantacle <note>A physical talisman or disk inscribed with magical symbols</note> to be used as a breastplate <note>original: "lamen"</note> on the magician’s chest. Prayers <note>original: "orations"</note> similar to those in the Key of Solomon <note>A famous grimoire or book of magic traditionally attributed to King Solomon</note> are given for putting on the robes before the ceremony.*

*In many ways, Abano’s instructions for calling upon spirits <note>original: "invocation"</note> are*

<summary>This page details the life of Peter of Abano, his conflicts with the Inquisition, and the structure of his magical treatise, the Heptameron. It outlines the ritual requirements of the text, including the creation of magic circles, planetary hours, and the preparation of ritual tools and garments.</summary>
<keywords>Peter of Abano, Heptameron, Inquisition, Picatrix, magic circle, planetary angels, ritual magic, grimoire, lamen, pantacle</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Peter of Abano's work, specifically describing the structure of the Heptameron and its practical application, before moving on to the fourth treatise in the collection by Georg Pictorius.</meta>

*It is much more straightforward than most grimoires <note>A textbook of magic, typically including instructions on how to create magical objects like talismans and amulets, how to perform magical spells, charms, and divination, and how to summon or invoke supernatural entities</note>; the emphasis is on the careful recitation of the conjurations <note>Ritual summons or spells</note> in Latin to the spirits of the elements. These conjurations are primarily Kabbalistic <note>original: "qabalistic"; referring to Jewish mystical tradition</note> words of power, with the occasional word of Greek or Gnostic <note>Relating to early Christian-era mystical knowledge</note> origin appearing every so often. Following these, specific instructions are given for each day of the week, beginning with the magic circle to be used, the name of the angel for the day, his sigil <note>A symbol representing a spirit or magical force</note>, planet, and zodiac sign, along with his ministers and the names of power to be used at each cardinal direction <note>original: "quarter"</note>, which precede a specific conjuration suitable for that day. Peter also wrote a work on geomancy <note>A method of divination using marks on the ground or patterns formed by earth or sand</note> that is a classic in its field, neatly complementing the two studies of the subject included in this collection.*

### *IV. Introduction to the Nature of Spirits <note>original: "Isagoge... Of the Nature of Spirits"</note> — Georg Pictorius Villinganus (c. 1500–1569)*

*The fourth treatise is an introductory discourse on the nature of the spirits that exist in the sublunary sphere <note>The region of the cosmos below the moon, traditionally associated with the changing, earthly, and elemental realms</note>; their origin, names, duties <note>original: "offices"</note>, illusions, powers, prophecies, miracles, and how they may be expelled.*

*Pictorius began his career as a schoolmaster at Freiburg-im-Breisgau, where he became a Doctor of Medicine and a professor of medicine, before taking the position of physician at the archducal court at Ensisheim in Alsace. His first publications (1530) were medical works, commentaries, scholarly notes <note>original: "scholia"</note>, collections, and tables of medieval authors—such as Macer on herbs and Marbod on gems—or classical writers like Hippocrates, Pliny, Aristotle, Galen, and Oppian. In 1563, Pictorius published the work translated here.*

*Much of Pictorius’ writing is a summary of earlier authors. Although often lacking in imagination, it is quite faithful to the originals and conscientiously cited. As such, Pictorius’ writings are valuable for accurately showing the longevity and survival of the magical ideas he presents.*

*In the Introduction <note>original: "Isagoge"</note>, Pictorius cites Apuleius, Augustine <note>original: "Augustina"</note>, Iamblichus, Pliny, Saxo Grammaticus, Psellus (whom he calls a necromancer <note>A magician who communicates with the dead or spirits</note>), Peter Lombard, Trithemius, and Marcus Cherrhonesus (whom he refers to as a ‘distinguished devotee of demons’).*

*The Introduction <note>original: "Isagoge"</note> is organized as a conversation between the*

<summary>This page describes the practical, planetary structure of Peter of Abano's Heptameron and introduces Georg Pictorius, a 16th-century physician whose work "Isagoge" summarizes classical and medieval beliefs about spirits.</summary>
<keywords>Heptameron, Peter of Abano, Georg Pictorius, conjurations, Kabbalah, geomancy, sublunary sphere, necromancy, Renaissance magic</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the fourth treatise in the collection, Georg Pictorius’s work on the nature of spirits, explaining his philosophical and theological framework.</meta>

*...the classical Greek twins Castor and Pollux. The argument attempts to prove that the word daemon <note>In classical Greek, a "daemon" was a benevolent or neutral spirit, though the word later became the root for the modern "demon."</note> "is not a horrible or hateful name, but the name of one who serves, helps, or assists another, and whom Pliny calls a god." This book is an important essay for its time because it seeks to differentiate between the evil spirits of Christian theology and Greek daemons, who were categorized into three degrees, ranging from spirits of the air up to what Homer called gods. It was certainly important then—and of interest to practical magicians today—to distinguish the differences between these types of spirits. This book forms a bridge between the magical theory of Iamblichus of Chalcis <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote on the mysteries and theurgy (divine magic).</note> and the grimoires <note>Textbooks of magic used to summon spirits or create charms.</note> such as Peter de Abano’s Heptameron. Using the form of a dialogue, Pictorius is able to set forth the objections of the Church and counter each one by referring to various authorities—including Scripture, Peter Lombard, Sappho, and Diocletian—concerning the position of spirits in the world and the form and nature of their bodies.*

*The doctrine that spirits are guardians of the treasures of the earth—gems and precious metals as well as buried treasure—is examined in detail, for the discovery of buried treasure by the use of spirits was an all-consuming interest. In the days before banks, the burial of treasure was quite a common occurrence, and its discovery by accident or magic was almost as common. Various acts of the devil and his ministers on earth, together with accounts of apparitions and the natural occurrence of spirits, are then invoked with long anecdotes from Pliny.*

*In many ways, this book is an excellent summary of all the diverse influences, both Christian and pagan, which came together during the late Middle Ages and early Renaissance to form the magical tradition of the West. It is, in fact, almost a bibliography of source books on magic and stories about demons, as well as being an insight into the attitude of the period toward magic.*

*Pictorius dwells at some length upon the different types of divination <note>The practice of seeking knowledge of the future or the unknown by supernatural means.</note>, all of which he attributes to the agency of the devil. He goes into detail about the different demons, distinguishing between northern and southern sublunar <note>Existing in the region below the moon; earthly or atmospheric spirits.</note> demons, accusers <note>original: "criminatores"</note> and scouts <note>original: "exploratores"</note>, and tempters <note>original: "tentatores"</note> and deceivers <note>original: "insidiatores"</note> (who accompany each person as their <term>evil genius</term> <note>A personal spirit or "guiding influence" thought to drive a person toward bad actions; the opposite of a "good genius."</note>). Pictorius’s arsenal against spirits is very traditional and includes...*

<summary>This page introduces Georg Pictorius’s treatise on spirits, explaining his attempt to reconcile classical Greek concepts of "daemons" with Christian theology. It highlights the period's obsession with using spirits to find buried treasure and Pictorius’s categorization of different demonic ranks.</summary>
<keywords>Georg Pictorius, daemon, Pliny the Elder, Iamblichus, divination, sublunar demons, buried treasure, demonology, Renaissance magic</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the works of Georg Pictorius and moves into a discussion of Gerard of Cremona’s work on geomancy and the magical treatise known as the Arbatel.</meta>

*...the name of Jesus, fire, the sword, insults, incense smoke <note>original: "suffumigations"</note>, the ringing of bells, and even the shaking of keys and clashing of arms!*

*Unfortunately, Pictorius took a very harsh stance against witches and argued that they should all be put to death. This was not so much for their non-Christian or harmful activities, but for having sexual intercourse with spirits, which Pictorius believed were both fertile and capable of reproduction!*

*Johannes Weir, the pupil of Agrippa <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, a famous 16th-century occultist and polymath.</note> already mentioned above, spoke rather dismissively of Pictorius’s "shallow <note>original: "jejune"</note> writing… concerning earthly <note>original: "sublunar"</note> matters." It may be that the publication of this work by Pictorius alongside the alleged Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy by his master prompted Weir to deny that the Fourth Book was actually written by Agrippa.*

### *V. On Astronomical Geomancy—Gerard of Cremona (1114–1187)*

*Gerard of Cremona was perhaps one of the greatest translators of the twelfth century, having been responsible for translating the Almagest of Ptolemy (the most influential book on astrology of that era) into Latin, along with works by Aristotle, Euclid, Galen, Avicenna, and many more. Working in Toledo, Spain, he is credited by his students with translating most of the Greek and Arabic texts available in the Middle Ages—a total of 71 different texts, some of which were massive in size. Some critics have suggested that our current text was translated by Gerard of Sabbionetta, from a town near Cremona, but this seems unlikely.*

*Astronomical Geomancy <note>A method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil, rocks, or sand.</note> offers a different system of geomancy than the one outlined by Agrippa in the first treatise of this volume. Although the points are generated in the same way, the figures are immediately converted into their planetary or zodiacal equivalents and placed into a horoscope.*

*The majority of the treatise is devoted to questions regarding the different astrological "Houses" and how to interpret them based on the geomantically generated planets and signs occupying those houses.*

### *VI. On Magic—Arbatel*

*This small treatise on the magic of the ancients was published in Basel in 1575 as Arbatel, On the Magic of the Ancients <note>original: "De Magia Veterum"</note>. Despite the fact that the word "Arbatel" is also printed in Hebrew, it is obvious that the author was a Christian, given the frequent inclusion of pious sentiments and Biblical quotes. Because of...*

<summary>This page discusses the controversial views of Georg Pictorius on witchcraft and introduces the historical background of Gerard of Cremona’s astronomical geomancy and the Arbatel, a late 16th-century magical text.</summary>
<keywords>Georg Pictorius, Johannes Weir, Agrippa, Gerard of Cremona, Astronomical Geomancy, Arbatel, astrology, translation, Toledo, Medieval magic</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the magical works, specifically focusing on the origins and structure of the Arbatel and the history of its English translation by Robert Turner.</meta>

*Because the 30th and 31st Aphorisms mention obscure details of Italian history, the author might have been from that country—perhaps even a judge <note>original: "magistrate"</note> from Naples. However, the name "Arbatel" is likely not a pen name, but rather the name of an angel who reveals secrets from one of the four corners of the world.*

*This book is said to contain nine volumes <note>original: "tomes"</note>. The first volume, containing 49 Aphorisms on the general rules of magic, serves as the <term>Isagoge</term> <note>An introductory treatise or manual.</note> or introduction. It is the only part included in the 1575 edition and in this current translation.*

*Although the book's preface claims there are eight more volumes, none of them seem to have survived <note>original: "extant"</note>.*

*While it is probable that the first volume was the only one actually written, it frequently overlaps with topics that were supposed to be covered in the later parts.*

*For example, the 16th Aphorism discusses the seven Olympic Spirits and their territories <note>original: "provinces"</note>. (In the original text, the number of territories is listed as 186, with 32 ruled by the spirit Bethor. This is likely a printing error for 196 and 42 respectively, because in the system established later, each Olympic spirit rules seven fewer territories than the one before it). Each Olympic Spirit is said to rule for an era of 490 years; the spirit currently in power is Ophiel. Each spirit is also assigned various planetary associations, a <term>sigil</term> <note>A unique symbolic seal or signature used in magical practice.</note>, a list of specific powers, and a count of the spirit legions under their command.*

*It is interesting to note that a recently published book of magic <note>original: "grimoire"</note> called "The Secret Grimoire of Turiel" by Marius Malchus—which was allegedly discovered in Las Palmas in 1927 as a Latin manuscript dated 1518—appears to be based on this specific Aphorism, with extra details pulled from other parts of this volume.*

### Translation

*This is the first English translation of this collection of six academic essays <note>original: "treatises"</note>. A. E. Waite—who was usually very quick to criticize the skills of other translators—gave Robert Turner's work the highest praise when he wrote: "I shall depart from my usual custom of translating directly from the original source <note>original: "at first hand"</note> and make use... of the version by Robert Turner, which is quite accurate and has, furthermore, a pleasant antique feel." This translation was published in 1655, just one year before*

<keywords>Arbatel, Olympic Spirits, Bethor, Ophiel, Robert Turner, A. E. Waite, Isagoge, grimoire, sigil, Naples, magic history</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the history of magical literature, specifically discussing Robert Turner’s 17th-century translations and his efforts to distinguish "natural philosophy" from common sorcery.</meta>

*This translation of Paracelsus’s work, **Of the Supreme Mysteries of Nature**, which is published as **The Archidoxes of Magic**, is Volume 2 of the **Source Works of Medieval and Renaissance Magic** series.*

*Robert Turner’s contribution to the spread of magical knowledge in the common language <note>original: "vernacular"</note> in seventeenth-century England is significant, as he made some of the finest occult writings of the time available to the general public.*

*Agrippa’s **Three Books of Occult Philosophy** were also translated by Robert Turner, but until now they have not been reprinted, except for a "modernized" version of the first volume only. That version was published by Willis Whitehead in 1897 and has been reprinted several times since. It was mistakenly titled to give the impression that it contained all three books. In fact, it contains only the first book, which is concerned mainly with "natural" or sympathetic magic <note>Magic based on the idea that things act on each other at a distance through a spiritual connection or likeness.</note>. The two other volumes, which deal more closely with spiritual correspondences, the Qabalah <note>A system of Jewish mysticism often adapted by Renaissance scholars.</note>, invocation, and evocation, have not actually been reprinted in English since Turner’s original translation. It is the publisher's hope to release all three as Volume 3 in the current series.*

*Turner felt obligated to defend magic according to the standards of his own time, using Biblical and Classical quotes. He was careful to point out the difference between evil sorcerers <note>original: "malefici"</note> or poisoners <note>original: "venefici"</note>—who relied mostly on low cunning, fear, and poisons—and the dedicated student of "natural philosophy" <note>The historical term for the study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science.</note>, for whom the gates of experimental science were just beginning to open. The latter took all of nature as his territory, including things that seemed to be beyond nature or supernatural. Such a student could be equally interested in spirits or in the light-bending and image-projecting properties of glass lenses (as was the scholar Dr. John Dee). To him, both fields were equally part of his studies—or, if you prefer, both were equally beyond the reach of scientific certainty. Both were still considered miraculous.*

*Today, only spirits remain "miraculous," as the famous <term>burning glass</term> <note>A lens used to concentrate sunlight to create fire; a marvel of early optics.</note> has long since been explained by known science. But in a way, modern opinion is less open to experiment, less honest, and less open-minded than it was in the late Renaissance. Today, the idea of spirits is no longer even debated; it has simply been thrown onto the scrap heap.*

*However, since the beginning of the last century, an insidious...*

<summary>This page discusses Robert Turner's vital role in translating magical texts into English and clarifies the publishing history of Agrippa’s works while exploring the Renaissance connection between early science and magic.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Turner, Paracelsus, Agrippa, natural philosophy, John Dee, burning glass, sorcery vs. science, book history</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface by Stephen Skinner, discussing the modern psychological interpretation of magic versus the traditional focus on external spiritual entities.</meta>

*A phenomenon <note>original: "phenomena"</note> has begun to develop within magic itself—a phenomenon roughly equivalent to Robert Turner’s seventeenth-century attempts to make magic acceptable in the eyes of his religiously minded contemporaries. In some ways, this modern growth is an extension of that religiously oriented defense of magic, except that it is actually a scientifically oriented defense; defenders <note>original: "apologists"</note> have always felt a compulsion to reshape their arguments to fit the fashion of their own time.*

*Magic is basically the science and art of causing change to occur in accordance with Will, through spiritual forces <note>original: "agencies"</note> that have been called forth <note>original: "evoked or invoked"</note> from either the microcosm (the inner world: man’s own usually subconscious sources of power) or the macrocosm (the outer world: the universe). This definition also includes "magical technologies," such as the various forms of divination, which are necessary additions <note>original: "adjuncts"</note> to the above. It is the loss of the latter half (the macrocosmic part) of the definition that has allowed modern defenders of magic to suggest that all changes are internal and psychological—aimed at improving the inner person and enabling them to move beyond <note>original: "transcend"</note> their personality's limitations and achieve enlightenment. The early stages of this phenomenon can easily be explained away in terms of Jungian psychology <note>Refers to the theories of Carl Jung, focusing on the "collective unconscious" and the process of "individuation" or becoming a complete individual.</note>—the pursuit of personal wholeness <note>original: "integration"</note>. The later stages can be explained by citing religious and mystical precedents, thereby avoiding the true essence of magic as it was practiced before the nineteenth century.*

*This is not to say that there is not great value in a Jungian or Reichian <note>Refers to Wilhelm Reich, a psychoanalyst who believed in a physical life force called "orgone."</note> approach to magic; it is simply that such views leave a large portion of magic unaccounted for.*

*One might say that magic has developed over the last couple of centuries, but how can a subject "develop" if it narrows its focus so much that it throws out the majority of its theory (for the belief in external entities was a central belief) without actually improving its techniques?*

*It is for this reason that this book and others in the same series are being republished: to bring forward some of the best thinking in the field before it is smothered by a mass of "scientific" rationalization—which is just as oppressive in its own way as religious bias.*

->— *Stephen Skinner*<-
->*London, March 1978*<-

<summary>In this concluding preface, Stephen Skinner argues that modern "scientific" or psychological interpretations of magic ignore its traditional focus on external spiritual entities (the macrocosm). He explains that this series of books aims to preserve original magical theory before it is rewritten by modern rationalization.</summary>
<keywords>magic, Will, spiritual agencies, microcosm, macrocosm, divination, Jungian psychology, integration, Reichian, external entities, Stephen Skinner</keywords>

# The PREFACE
## To the Unprejudiced Reader.

<margin>Pliny, Book 30, *Natural History*</margin>
<image-desc>An ornamental drop-cap letter 'A' decorated with floral scrollwork and geometric patterns.</image-desc>
Just as the fall of man made himself and all other creatures subject to vanity, so, because of this, the most noble and excellent arts with which the rational soul was endowed have been brought to corruption by the rusting decay of time. For <term>Magic</term> <note>original: "Magick"; here referring to "natural magic," the study of the hidden powers of nature and the divine, rather than modern stage magic.</note> itself, which the ancients contemplated so divinely, is slandered by being associated with the label of all diabolical sorceries. Regarding this art, Mirandola <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous Italian Renaissance philosopher who sought to reconcile magic and Christianity.</note> says: "Few understand it, many criticize it, and just as dogs always bark at strangers, so do many condemn and hate the things they do not understand" <note>original Latin: *Pauci intelligunt, multi reprehendunt, & sicut canes ignotos semper allatrant*</note>. 

There are many men who abhor the very name and word *Magus* <note>A practitioner of magic; originally a Persian term for a priest or wise man.</note> because of Simon Magus <note>A biblical figure (Acts 8:9–24) who practiced sorcery and tried to buy the power of the Holy Spirit from the Apostles.</note>, who was indeed not a *Magus* but a <term>Goetist</term> <note>original: "Goes"; a practitioner of "Goetia," or low sorcery involving the invocation of evil spirits.</note>—that is, someone familiar with evil spirits—who usurped that title. 

However, magic and witchcraft are very different sciences. Pliny, being ignorant of this distinction, mocked it. For Nero—as Pliny says—had the most excellent magicians of the East sent to him by Tiridates, King of Armenia (who held that kingdom through him). Yet after long study and labor, Nero found the art to be altogether ridiculous. 

In truth, witchcraft and sorcery are works performed merely by the devil. In accordance with some covenant made with a human, the devil acts through these people as his instruments to accomplish his evil ends. The histories of all ages, peoples, and countries, as well as the Holy Scriptures, provide us with various examples of these.

But *Magus* is originally a Persian word, by which...

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>is</meta>

<summary>This preface argues that true, ancient magic is a noble and misunderstood art, distinguishing it from diabolical witchcraft and sorcery. The author blames historical figures like Simon Magus and misconceptions by writers like Pliny for the art's poor reputation.</summary>
<keywords>Magick, Magus, Goetia, Witchcraft, Sorcery, Pico della Mirandola, Pliny the Elder, Nero, Simon Magus, Natural History</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of a "Magus" (magician) started on the previous page, distinguishing the holy origins of the title from its later association with sorcery.</meta>

# The Preface.

...is described as someone who is completely engaged in divine matters. As *Plato* claims, the art of *magic* is the art of worshipping God, and the *Persians* called their gods *Magos*. Therefore, *Apollonius* <note>original: "Apollonius"; likely Apollonius of Tyana, a first-century Greek philosopher and reputed miracle-worker.</note> says that *Magus* is either a god by nature or a servant of the gods <note>original Greek: ὁ κατὰ φύσιν Θεὸς, or θεραπευτὴς Θεῶν</note>. That is, *Magus* is a name sometimes used for one who is a god by nature, and sometimes for one who is in the service of God. 

It is used in this latter sense in Matthew 2:1–2, when the wise men <note>The Magi who followed the star to Bethlehem.</note> came to worship Jesus. This is the first and highest kind of magic, which is called "divine magic." The *Latins* titled these people *sapientes*, or wise men; for the fear and worship of God is the beginning of knowledge. These wise men the *Greeks* call *philosophers*; among the *Egyptians*, they were termed *priests*; the *Hebrews* termed them <term>Cabalists</term> <note>original: "Cabalistos"; followers of Kabbalah, a school of Jewish mysticism.</note>, *prophets*, *scribes*, and *Pharisees*; among the *Babylonians*, they were distinguished by the name *Chaldeans*; and by the *Persians*, they were called *magicians*. 

One author, speaking of *Sosthenes* <note>An ancient Persian sage often cited in classical texts as an authority on magic and religion.</note> (one of the ancient magicians), uses these words: "He honors the true God with his deserved majesty and acknowledges that the angels are God’s ministers, yet he knows how to assist in the true worship of God; he also exposes earthly, wandering demons who are enemies of humanity" <note>original Latin: *Et verum Deum merita majestate prosequitur, & angelos ministros Dei, sed veri ejus venerationi novit assistere; idem demonas prodit terrenos, Vagos, humanitatis inimicos*</note>. 

Thus, *Sosthenes* ascribes proper majesty to the true God and acknowledges that his angels are ministers and messengers who attend the worship of the true God. He also taught that there are earthly and wandering devils who are enemies to mankind.

So, the word *Magus* itself signifies a student of divine and heavenly sciences. However, under the general name of *magic*, all unlawful arts are often included—such as <term>necromancy</term> <note>The practice of communicating with the dead.</note> and witchcraft, and any arts achieved through an alliance with the devil where he is a participant.

<summary>The author defines "Magus" as a title for a divine philosopher or servant of God across various ancient cultures, distinguishing "divine magic" from forbidden practices like necromancy and witchcraft.</summary>
<keywords>Magus, divine magic, Sosthenes, Apollonius of Tyana, etymology of magic, witchcraft, Chaldeans, Cabalists</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's categorization of magic. Having previously discussed "divine magic," the text now distinguishes between harmful sorcery (associated with witches) and the intellectual science of astrology, which the author links to the biblical patriarch Abraham.</meta>

# The Preface.

These witches and necromancers are also called *malefici* <note>original Latin: "malefici"; evildoers or those who perform harmful magic.</note> or *venefici* <note>original Latin: "venefici"; poisoners or sorcerers.</note>; sorcerers or poisoners. Witches are rightly called by this name because, without the Art of Magic, they indeed use the help of the devil himself to do mischief. They practice mixing the powder of dead bodies with other things prepared by the help of the devil. At other times, they make pictures of wax or clay, or use other methods (acting, as it were, *sacramentaliter* <note>original Latin: "sacramentaliter"; in a sacramental manner, meaning the physical object acts as a ritual sign or tool for the devil's work</note>) to bring about those things which the devil achieves by other means. Such were—and to this day partly, if not altogether, are—the corruptions that have made the very name of "Magic" hateful. These practitioners have chiefly sought, as is the manner of all impostures, to counterfeit the highest and most noble part of it.

A second kind of Magic is Astrology, which judges the events of things to come—both natural and human—by observing and understanding the motions and influences of the stars upon these lower elements.

*Philo Judaeus* <note>A Jewish philosopher from Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) who sought to harmonize Greek philosophy with Jewish scripture.</note> affirms that by this part of Magic or Astrology, together with the motions of the stars and other heavenly bodies, *Abraham* discovered the knowledge of the true God while he lived in *Chaldea*. As *Damascene* <note>John of Damascus (c. 675–749), a Syrian monk and priest known for his theological writings.</note> says, "He knew the Creator by the contemplation of the creature" <note>original Latin: "Qui Contemplatione Creaturarum, cognovit Creatorem"</note>. *Josephus* <note>Flavius Josephus (37–100 CE), a Romano-Jewish historian.</note> reports of *Abraham* that he instructed the Egyptians in arithmetic and astronomy; before *Abraham*'s arrival, they knew none of these sciences.

"Abraham, the most excellent of all in holiness and wisdom, first taught the Chaldeans, then the Phoenicians, and finally the Egyptian priests, Astrology and Divine matters" <note>original Latin: "Abraham sanctitate & sapientia omnium præstantissimus, primum Caldæos, deinde Phœnices, demum Egyptios Sacerdotes, Astrologia & Divina docuerit."</note>. *Abraham*, the holiest and wisest of men, did first teach the Chaldeans...

<summary>The author distinguishes "true magic" from the harmful practices of witches and necromancers, while defining astrology as a legitimate science used by Abraham to find God and educate the ancient world.</summary>
<keywords>witchcraft, necromancy, sorcery, astrology, Abraham, Philo Judaeus, Josephus, Chaldea, Egyptian history, natural philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the history of magic and astrology, moving from the Chaldeans to the Phoenicians and Egyptians, before defining natural magic and the wisdom of Zoroaster.</meta>

# The Preface.

[Chalde]ans, then the Phoenicians, and lastly the Egyptian priests, [who studied] astrology and divine knowledge.

Without doubt, *Hermes Trismegistus* <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure portrayed as a combination of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth; he was credited with writing the Hermetic corpus, a series of sacred texts on philosophy and magic.</note>, that divine magician and philosopher—who (as some say) lived long before *Noah*—attained much divine knowledge of the Creator through the study of magic and astrology, as his writings, which remain with us to this day, testify.

The third kind of magic contains the entire philosophy of nature. This science brings to light the innermost virtues and extracts them from nature's hidden bosom for human use. These are the "virtues hidden in the center of the center" (*virtutes in centro centri latentes* <note>original Latin: "virtutes in centro centri latentes"</note>), according to the chemists <note>original: "Chymists"; here referring to alchemists or early chemical philosophers.</note>. Of this sort were *Albertus* <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a German Dominican friar and philosopher known for his work in the natural sciences.</note>, *Arnoldus de Villa Nova* <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), an alchemist and physician.</note>, *Raymond* <note>Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and logician associated with alchemy.</note>, *Bacon* <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1219–1292), an English philosopher and Franciscan friar who emphasized the study of nature through experience.</note>, and others.

The magic these men practiced is defined this way: *Magic is the connection of natural active agents and passive subjects, corresponding to each other, performed by a wise man to produce effects that are wonderful to those who do not know their causes.* <note>original Latin: "Magia est connexio a viro sapiente agentium per naturam cum patientibus, sibi, congruenter respondentibus, ut inde opera prodeant, non sine eorum admiratione qui causam ignorant."</note>

In all these matters, *Zoroaster* <note>The ancient Persian prophet and founder of Zoroastrianism, often regarded by Renaissance thinkers as the original "Magus."</note> was well-learned, especially in the first and the highest forms of magic. In his oracles, he confesses God to be the first and the highest. He believes in the Trinity, which he did not try to investigate through natural knowledge alone. He speaks of angels and of paradise, confirms the immortality of the soul, and teaches truth, faith, hope, and love while discussing the self-denial and charity of the *Magi* <note>original: "Magi"; a term for the priestly class of ancient Persia, from which the word "magic" is derived.</note>.

<summary>The author traces the lineage of divine and natural magic through historical figures like Hermes Trismegistus and Zoroaster, while defining "natural magic" as a scientific endeavor to harness the hidden properties of the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, natural magic, philosophy of nature, Zoroaster, Magi, alchemy, Albertus Magnus, Roger Bacon, active and passive agents</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the history of magic and philosophy, specifically referencing the teachings of Zoroaster and defining magic as the highest form of natural science.</meta>

# The Preface.

Concerning this *Zoroaster*, *Eusebius*—in his *Theology of the Phoenicians*—quotes *Zoroaster's* own words: "He writes this word for word," says *Eusebius*: "God is the first, incorruptible, eternal, unbegotten, without parts, most like himself, the guide of all good, expecting no reward, the best, the wisest, the father of right, having learned justice without teaching, perfect, wise by nature, the only inventor of it" <note>original Latin: "Hæc ad verbum scribit... Deus primus, incorruptibilium, sempiternus, ingenitus, expers partium, sibiipsi simillimus, bonorum omnium auriga, munera non expectans, optimus, prudentissimus, pater juris, sine doctrina justitiam perdoctus, natura perfectus, sapiens, sacræ naturæ unicus inventor." Zoroaster was an ancient Persian prophet, but in this period, many "Hermetic" and mystical texts were falsely attributed to him.</note>. Thus said *Zoroaster*, word for word.

So, a magician is nothing other than a worshiper and interpreter of divine things <note>original Latin: "divinorum cultor & interpres"</note>; and the art itself is nothing other than the absolute perfection of natural philosophy <note>original Latin: "quam Naturalis Philosophiæ absoluta consummatio." Natural philosophy was the study of the physical world and nature, the precursor to modern science.</note>. Nevertheless, there is a mixture of good with evil in all things, of falsehood with truth, and of corruption with purity. The good, the truth, and the purity in every category should be embraced. For example, in the ancient practice of worshipping God by sacrifice, no man who knew God among the elders stopped worshipping the God of all power or condemned that kind of worship just because the devil was also adored through images like *Baal*, *Dagon*, *Ashtoreth* <note>original: "Astaroth"; a Phoenician goddess often associated with fertility.</note>, *Chemosh* <note>The national god of the Moabites.</note>, *Jupiter*, *Apollo*, and the like.

Neither did the abuse of astrology frighten *Abraham* (if we believe the most ancient and religious writers) away from observing the motions and natures of the heavenly bodies. Nor can it discourage <note>original: "dehort"</note> wise and learned

<meta>catchword: men</meta>

<summary>This page defines a "magician" as a student of divine things and magic as the perfection of natural philosophy, using the ancient wisdom of Zoroaster to describe the nature of God. It argues that the existence of pagan idolatry does not invalidate the legitimate study of astrology or divine worship practiced by figures like Abraham.</summary>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface by Robert Turner, finishing a thought from the previous page regarding the errors of those who worship the heavens rather than the Creator. It addresses potential critics and categorizes the different types of readers.</meta>

### The Preface.

...men in these days from attributing those virtues, influences, and inclinations to the stars and other lights of heaven, which God has given to those, his glorious creatures.

I must expect some slanders <note>original: "calumnies and obtrectations"</note> against this work from malicious, prejudiced men and those who lazily affect ignorance—with whom this age swarms. But the voices of the snake and the goose sound exactly the same <note>The author is comparing the "hissing" of malicious critics to the meaningless noise of animals.</note>. However, our stomachs are no longer so queasy and delicate after feeding for so long on solid theology <note>original: "Divinity"</note>. Nor are we so suspicious <note>original: "umbragious"</note> and easily startled—having been enlightened for so long in God's path—that we should relapse into that childish age when *Aristotle's Metaphysics* was forbidden to be read by a council in *France*.

Instead, I encourage the reader to have a charitable opinion of this work, with my Christian protestation of an innocent purpose. I also entreat the reader to follow the advice of *Tabaeus*: "Let those who litigate both be seen in your sight as wicked and guilty" <note>original Latin: "Qui litigant, sint ambo in conspectu tuo mali & rei." This suggests that in an argument, both sides often share the blame.</note>. And if there is any scandal in this enterprise of mine, it is taken by others, not given by me. I find comfort in that axiom of *Hermes Trismegistus*: "He who is pious practices philosophy to the highest degree" <note>original Latin: "Qui pius est, summe philosophatur." Hermes Trismegistus was a legendary figure associated with the origins of alchemy and magic.</note>. Therefore, I present this work without disguise and offer it to all who possess candor and impartiality. 

As one observer notes, there are four sorts of readers: 
*   **Sponges**, who absorb everything without distinction; 
*   **Hourglasses**, who receive information and pour it out just as fast; 
*   **Bags**, which retain only the dregs of spices and let the wine escape; 
*   and **Sieves**, which retain only the best. 

There are some readers of that last sort, and to them I present this *Occult Philosophy*, knowing they may reap good from it. As for those who are severe against it, I hope they will pardon my opinion: that their severity proceeds from their own inner guilt. Allow me to apply the words of *Ennodius*: "It is the nature of personal wickedness to believe others deserve what they themselves deserve" <note>Magnus Felix Ennodius (c. 473–521) was a bishop and poet; his sentiment here is that the guilty judge others harshly to avoid feeling alone in their sin.</note>. Indeed, the only comfort the guilty have is to find no one else innocent. But it is my desire that, among others, this work may find some acceptance.

->London, August 31, 1654. <note>original: "ult. Aug." refers to the *ultimo* or last day of August.</note><-
->**Robert Turner.**<-

<meta>catchword: To</meta>

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, Robert Turner defends his work on occult philosophy against critics, argues that his intent is pious and Christian, and identifies his ideal audience as discerning readers who seek the "best" knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Turner, Occult Philosophy, Hermes Trismegistus, Aristotle, Ennodius, 17th-century readers, censorship, piety and philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page contains a commendatory poem written by a friend (identified by the initials I.P.B.) in honor of Robert Turner's translation of Cornelius Agrippa's work. It transitions from Turner's own preface to praise from his peers.</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal decorative border composed of a row of fleurons or printer's ornaments.</image-desc>

# To his special friend Mr. R. Turner, on 
## his wise translation of 
# Cornelius Agrippa.

*Like someone just waking from a trance,*
*Amazed by strange sights, whose hidden fears*
*Are barely gone; yet doubting whether he*
*Can trust his eyes, he remains steadfastly*
*Fixed on those objects; just like him I stand,*
*Caught in amazement to see that one can*
*By art come near the gods, who far excel*
*The angels who dwell in those bright celestial spheres.*
*See Agrippa climbing the high skies,*
*Talking with gods; and then soon he pries*
*Into the earth's deep cabinet <note>original: "cabinet"; here meaning a private room or storehouse of secrets</note>. Just as for Mercury <note>The Roman messenger god, frequently associated with alchemy and the mediation between the divine and mortal realms.</note>,*
*All kinds of spirits are willing subjects,*
*And his book provides even more than this. But we*
*Blind mortals could in no way be led to see*
*That light without a candle <note>original: "taper"</note>. Therefore, you to us*
*Must be both an Agrippa and an Oedipus <note>In this context, Oedipus refers to a solver of riddles and mysteries, as he famously solved the riddle of the Sphinx.</note>.*
*Agrippa appears once again, by you*
*Pulled out of the ashes of antiquity.*
*Let squint-eyed envy waste away, while you*
*Wear crowns of praise on your deserving brow.*

->I.P.B. Cambridge <note>original Latin: "Cantabrigiæ".</note><-

<summary>This page features a commendatory poem praising Robert Turner for his translation of Cornelius Agrippa, characterizing Turner as a "solver of riddles" who has brought Agrippa's ancient wisdom back to life.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Turner, Cornelius Agrippa, translation, occultism, Cambridge, Mercury, Oedipus, antiquity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the series of commendatory poems praising Robert Turner for his English translation of Cornelius Agrippa’s occult works. This specific poem is written by "W. P." of St. John's College, Cambridge.</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal decorative border consisting of twenty repeating woodcut floral or fleur-de-lis ornaments.</image-desc>

## To his ingenious friend Mr. Turner,
## upon his Translation.

> Thrice-noble soul! Renowned epitome
> Of learning and occult philosophy;
> Who imparts unknown geomancy <note>original: "Geomancie"; a method of divination interpreting patterns formed by earth, stones, or sand.</note>,
> With profound secrets of that abstruse art!
> To explain natural magic is your task,
> Not to unmask hell-born necromancy <note>original: "Necromancie"; the practice of communicating with the spirits of the dead.</note>;
> Exposing mysteries to public view,
> That before now were known to very few.
> You do not keep your knowledge to yourself
> (As base, covetous misars do their wealth <note>original: "pelf"; money or riches, often used with a connotation of being ill-gotten or worthless.</note>;
> Whose numerous bags of rust-eaten gold
> Profit no one, until they themselves are buried in the earth <note>original: "laid in mold"; a poetic way of saying buried in the ground.</note>)
> But being dedicated to the public good, you make
> All people share in the fruits of your labors.
> Therefore, if some fault-finding <note>original: "captious"</note> critic blames
> Your writings, surely then his judgment is flawed.
> Art has no hater but an empty head <note>original: "pate"</note>,
> Which can far better complain than imitate.
> No, even Zoilus or Momus <note>Zoilus was a Greek grammarian known for harsh criticism; Momus was the Greek god of mockery and censure. They represent the ultimate unfair critics.</note> will not dare
> To blame your translation, which is beyond compare
> And excellent. So that if a hundred tongues
> Mother Nature had bestowed, and lungs of brass;
> Yet to rightly trumpet <note>original: "buccinate"; from the Latin *buccinare*, meaning to proclaim loudly as if by a trumpet.</note> your praises,
> I would lack strength, as well as polished phrases.
> But if the gods will grant what I do crave,
> Then Enoch’s translation <note>In the Bible, Enoch did not die but was "translated" or taken directly by God into heaven. The poet is making a pun: because Turner translated a book, he deserves to be "translated" to heaven like Enoch.</note> shall you have.

->W. P. St. John's College, Cambridge<-

<summary>A commendatory poem by W. P. of Cambridge praising Robert Turner for sharing secret knowledge of "natural magic" and geomancy with the public, rather than hoarding it like a miser. The author defends Turner against potential critics and concludes with a pun on Enoch's biblical "translation" to heaven.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Turner, Occult Philosophy, Geomancy, Natural Magic, Necromancy, St. John's College Cambridge, Commendatory Verse, Translation</keywords>

# *To his friend the* Author, *on this his* Translation.

*Are there no* Sibyls <note>Ancient prophetesses who were believed to possess divine knowledge.</note> *or* Cassandra <note>A Trojan priestess cursed to utter true prophecies that were never believed.</note> *left?*
*Has* Apollo <note>The Greek and Roman god of prophecy, music, and the sun.</note> *ceased? Has sharp-fanged* Time *robbed*
*Us of the* Oracles? *Is* Dodona’s <note>original: "Dodan’s"; Dodona was an ancient Greek oracle where the rustling leaves of sacred oak trees were interpreted as the voice of Zeus/Jove.</note> *grove*
*Cut down? Does no word proceed from* Jove <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods.</note>
*Into the ears of mortals who inherit*
Tiresias’ <note>A famous blind prophet of Apollo in Thebes.</note> *soul, or the great* Calchas’ <note>The seer for the Greeks during the Trojan War.</note> *spirit?*
*What has become of the* Augurs <note>Ancient Roman religious officials who observed natural signs, especially the flight of birds, to interpret the will of the gods.</note> *who foretold*
Nature’s *intentions? Are the* Magi <note>Ancient Persian priests or "wise men" studied in astrology and magic.</note> *dead, who could*
*Tell what was done in every celestial* Sphere? *Shall we*
*Not know what is happening in the most remote* Country
*Without great travel? Can we not from here below see*
*The mind of the gods above? All of this is accomplished by you,*
Agrippa; *all their* Arts *are contained within you.*
*The* Art *that previously existed in various heads,*
*Is now collected into one* Monopoly <note>original: "Monopoly"; here used in the sense of a consolidated or unified source of knowledge.</note>.
*But all was in vain; we lacked an* Oedipus <note>The mythical Greek figure famous for solving the riddle of the Sphinx.</note>,
*Who should interpret the meaning for us:*
*This you accomplish with such skill,*
*Adding perhaps what the* Author *never saw;*
*So that we may say, You renew the* Art:
*To you the greater half of the praise is due.*

->J. B. of Cambridge <note>original: "Cantabrigiæ"; the Latin name for Cambridge.</note>.<-

## To the Author, on his Translation of Cornelius Agrippa.

*You are the Pallas* <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom, crafts, and strategic warfare.</note> *of Learning, if you were named a Goddess;*
*Your knowledge has explained that original prototype;*
*Nature, also striving to combine*
*Science and Learning in this form of yours,*
*Does not show it to us obscurely, but clearly shows*
*The knowledge of mysteries within you as if in a shrine.*
*It is by your permission that we have access*
*Into Geomancy;* <note>original: "Geomancy"; a method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or patterns formed by earth, rocks, or sand.</note> *which, unless*
*You had unmasked it, would have remained a mystery,*
*A task too difficult for mortals to explain.*
*Since you have preserved it from the Lethean* <note>original: "Lethæan"; referring to Lethe, the river of forgetfulness in the Greek underworld. To rescue something from "Lethean floods" means to save it from being forgotten by history.</note> *floods,*
*We will dedicate the laurel buds*
*To you: (with Phoebus* <note>Apollo, the Greek and Roman god of prophecy and the sun.</note> *dismissed) yours shall be*
*The Oracle, to which all people shall flee*
*In times of danger; your predictions shall*
*Enslave our willing hearts to whatever you command;*
*Yes, you shall be*
*Our sole Prophet, and we will be obedient to you.*

->*J. R.*<-

# To the Author, on his Translation of Cornelius Agrippa.

*Does Apollo* <note>original: "Phœbus"; the Greek and Roman god of prophecy and the sun.</note> *cease to answer our demands?*
*Or will he not accept from mortals’ hands*
*A sorrowful sacrifice?* <note>original: "Bidental"; in ancient Rome, a place struck by lightning that was consecrated by the sacrifice of a sheep (a bidens).</note> *And is the Sibyl's cave*
*Uninhabitable?* <note>original: "Inhabitable"; in Early Modern English, this often meant "uninhabitable."</note> *Or may Tiresias* <note>A famous blind prophet of Apollo in Greek mythology.</note> *have*
*No successor nor rival? How shall we*
*Then direct an Oedipus* <note>The Greek king who solved the riddle of the Sphinx; used here as a metaphor for someone capable of solving deep mysteries.</note> *to the world? If he*
*Adds incest to parricide, they are silent,*
*Those who could predict what things would surely come;*
*And they are silent who knew when to apply*
*To our body politic a purge and bloodletting.* <note>original: "Phlebotomy"; the medical practice of opening a vein to draw blood, used here metaphorically for "curing" the problems of the state.</note>
*How will bold thieves rob our treasures? Who shall*
*Regain lost goods, or by his charms recall*
*The guilty?* <note>original: "nocent"; an archaic term meaning harmful or criminal.</note> *This art is rescued* <note>original: "repriv'd"; reprieved or saved from execution/oblivion.</note> *by you;*
*In you the Magi* <note>Ancient wise men or magicians.</note> *seem to be revived.*
*Apollo is not mentally ill, Jupiter’s* <note>original: "Joves."</note> *doves* <note>A reference to the sacred doves of the Oracle at Dodona.</note> *are not dead,*
*The oracles have not ceased. Agrippa's bed—*
*(Like the Phoenix's* <note>original: "Arabian bird"; the mythical bird that burns and is reborn from its own ashes.</note> *self-built nest,*
*Which first proves to be her funeral urn, then her place of rebirth)* <note>original: "quickning rest"; "quickening" refers to coming to life.</note>
*Has produced you, who are surely more than his equal;*
*Otherwise, this art would have remained hidden,*
*A miracle to the unlearned,* <note>original: "vulgars"; the common or uneducated people.</note> *well known to no one,*
*Scarcely understood even by those with the deepest insight.*
*Therefore, I will conclude, since you explain him so well,*
*That the "younger brother" has the better brain.* <note>The poet suggests that the translator (the successor or "younger brother" to Agrippa) has surpassed the original author in clarity and intellect.</note>

->John Tomlinson,<-
->of St. John's College in Cambridge.<-

# To his good friend the Author, on his Translation of *Occult Philosophy* and Geomancy. <note>original: "Geomancie"</note>

*Most noble undertakings! As if Art*
*And Prudence should strike a bargain to share*
*Radiant brilliance:* <note>original: "Refulgent lustres"</note> *you send forth a ray*
*Which the noblest patrons could never display.*
*Well may Diana* <note>The Roman goddess of the moon and the hunt, often associated with mystery and hidden knowledge.</note> *love you and inspire*
*Your noble genius with heavenly* <note>original: "cœlestial"</note> *fire,*
*Whose sparkling imagination* <note>original: "Fancie"</note> *has more power to subdue,*
*And conquer sooner, than a magic spell.*
*The Author* <note>Referring to Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, the original writer of the Latin text.</note> *did not think (when he wrote the book)*
*That he would be surpassed by a loftier look,*
*Or be overtaken by a more triumphant style,* <note>original: "strein" (strain)</note>
*Which would elevate his then-most pleasant tone.* <note>original: "vein"</note>
*But seeing that a later descendant* <note>original: "progeny"</note>
*Has rescued his honor from obscurity,*
*Both shall live again, and let spectators know*
*Who best deserves the laurel wreath.* <note>original: "Lawrel bow"; a "bough" of laurel was used to crown victors and poets.</note>
*Nature and Art strive here to gain*
*The victory: and though he* <note>The original author, Agrippa.</note> *refuses to yield,*
*You have gained the advantage: though he triumphs in praise,*
*Yet may his ivy wait upon your laurel.* <note>original: "Ivie wait upon your Bays"; in classical symbolism, ivy and laurel (bays) were used for crowns. Ivy often represented the scholar or lower rank of fame, while laurel represented the highest achievement of the poet or victor.</note>

->M. S.<-
->*Cambridge.* <note>original: "Cantabrigiæ"; the Latin name for Cambridge, indicating the writer was likely a scholar at the University.</note><-

<summary>In this commendatory poem, a scholar from Cambridge named M. S. praises Robert Turner's translation of Agrippa's work, suggesting that the translation's beauty and clarity actually surpass the original Latin text.</summary>

## To the Author, on this his ingenious
### Translation of
# Cornelius Agrippa.

*What is it I see? Agrippa made to wear*
*An English clothing?* <note>original: "habit"; here referring to the English language as a "garment" for the ideas.</note> *Surely this is something rare.*
*Or are his Roman garments,* <note>Referring to the original Latin in which Agrippa wrote.</note> *by your Wit,*
*Translated to an English style so fit*
*To showcase him? For we see that you have*
*Enlightened his obscure Philosophy;*
*And that which remained so complex and difficult,*
*You have made accessible to every common mind.* <term>vulgar: from the Latin *vulgus*, meaning the general public or common people rather than a specialized scholarly elite.</term>
*If your light shines so clearly through such dark works,*
*How brilliant will it appear in your own original writings!*
*Then keep going, brave soul, to spread such rays*
*Of Learning through the world as may speak your praise.*
*And fear no critics: for you, by a spell,*
*Can force their tongues to stay silent behind their teeth.* <note>original: "within their teeth to dwell."</note>

->**John Tabor,**<-
->*of* St. John’s College *in* Cambridge.<-

<meta>Continuity: This page begins the treatise on Geomancy by Henry Cornelius Agrippa. The catchword "deprecations" at the bottom of the page indicates the text continues with a discussion of ritual prayers or pleas.</meta>

# Henry Cornelius Agrippa,
## On Geomancy.

<image-desc>A large historiated woodcut initial letter 'G' depicting a bearded male scholar or sage seated within an arched interior, holding an open book or scroll. The initial is surrounded by architectural flourishes and floral ornamentation.</image-desc>

Geomancy <term>Geomancy: a method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or the patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil, rocks, or sand</term> is an art of divination through which a judgment may be rendered by lot or destiny to every question on any subject whatsoever. However, the practice of this art consists especially in certain points from which specific figures are derived <note>original: "deducted"</note> according to the logic or rule of parity or disparity, similarity or difference. These figures are also related to celestial figures <note>original: "Cœlestiall Figures," referring to astrological charts or symbols</note>, assuming their natures and properties according to the course and forms of the Signs and Planets. 

Nevertheless, we must first consider that this kind of art can declare or show nothing of the truth unless it is rooted in some divine power <note>original: "radicall in some sublime vertue"</note>. The authors of this science have demonstrated that this power is twofold. The first part consists of religion and ceremonies. Therefore, they require the casting <note>original: "Projectings"</note> of the points of this art to be made with marks in the earth. For this reason, this art is assigned to the element of Earth, just as pyromancy <term>pyromancy: divination by fire</term> is to fire, and hydromancy <term>hydromancy: divination by water</term> is to the element of water. 

Then, because they judged the hand of the practitioner <note>original: "Projector or Worker"</note> to be most powerfully moved and directed by the spirits of the earth, they first used certain holy incantations and 

<summary>This page introduces Geomancy as a divinatory art based on earth-marks and astrological correspondences, emphasizing that its accuracy depends on divine power and specific religious rituals.</summary>
<keywords>Geomancy, Divination, Henry Cornelius Agrippa, Elements, Astrology, Earth Spirits, Pyromancy, Hydromancy</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa's explanation of the two powers that make Geomancy effective. The previous page ended while describing the first power: religious ceremonies and the ritual casting of points.</meta>

# Henry Cornelius Agrippa

prayers of plea <note>original: "deprecations"</note>, along with other rites and observations, which provoke and allure spirits of this nature to this end.

There is another power that directs and rules this Lot or Fortune, which resides in the very soul of the practitioner <note>original: "Projector"</note> when he is moved to this work by a great outpouring <note>original: "egresse"</note> of his own desire. For this art has a natural obedience to the soul itself; of necessity, it is effective and is moved toward that which the soul desires. This method is far more true and pure, and it does not matter where or how these points are cast <note>original: "projected"</note>. Therefore, this art has the same root <note>original: "Radix"</note> as the art of Astrological Questions <term>Astrological Questions: also known as Horary Astrology, the practice of casting an astrological chart for the specific moment a question is asked to find an answer</term>. This also cannot be verified in any other way unless it is performed with a constant and intense emotion <note>original: "excessive affection"</note> from the inquirer <note>original: "Querent"</note> himself.

Now, so that we may proceed to the practice <note>original: "Praxis"</note> of this art, it must first be known that there are only sixteen figures upon which this entire art is founded. You shall see these noted with their names in the following table.

<summary>Agrippa explains that Geomancy's accuracy relies either on spiritual rituals or the intense willpower and soul of the practitioner. He connects the art to horary astrology and introduces the sixteen fundamental geomantic figures.</summary>

<keywords>Geomancy, Henry Cornelius Agrippa, divination, astrology, horary questions, occult philosophy, sixteen figures</keywords>
